### [The Hackening: Justified Practical Advice And Open Questions Thread](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YA2r4rg6SpBtwRqtq/the-hackening-justified-practical-advice-and-open-questions)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In February 2020, LessWrong hosted  </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LwcKYR8bykM6vDHyo/coronavirus-justified-practical-advice-thread"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Coronavirus: Justified Practical Advice Thread</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> . It was a gathering place for people to share and debate small pieces of the overall survival policy and let the wisdom of crowds lead to better outcomes than anyone could get alone. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In that spirit, I notice </span><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/the-hackening" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Great AI Hackening</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is at least as imminent as covid was in January 2020, </span><a href="https://x.com/SecDuffy/status/2121351591106072679" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">maybe</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://x.com/MSFT365Status/status/2120938282204688468" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">closer</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. What specific things should people do to personally prepare? What are your unresolved cruxes that would let you answer that question?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All suggestions should be accompanied by reasoning but you don't need to be 100% convinced it's good advice, the whole point is to reason it out together.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YA2r4rg6SpBtwRqtq/the-hackening-justified-practical-advice-and-open-questions#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Psychologically Preparing for Short Timelines](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nKootiC6CuwuzLSou/psychologically-preparing-for-short-timelines)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In response to recent news from METR on possible RSI and likely 1-3 year timelines, I am </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">feeling it</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Heaviness in my chest, watery eyes, the works. I realized I'm currently not psychologically prepared for the world to get crazier than it already has.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbp1y9c17i1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbp1y9c17i1">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think most people reading this already know ~what's best for them and this post will mostly serve as a reminder. What follows is my current plan and advice for myself. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For me, the most important things are:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Morning Routine</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bedtime Routine</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Exercising</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Living meditation practice (ie throughout the day)</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And the most important thing to </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NOT</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> do is be on my phone/screens.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Note on getting "stuck": if you do feel emotionally awful and no IFS/CBT seems to work, I recommend "</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KvfYrMw3iDmvkMnaE/how-to-reset" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How to Reset</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"]</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Morning Routine</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I have a sunrise alarm clock (shines light to wake you up) and enjoy writing in the morning.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bedtime Routine</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At 8:30 an alarm goes off, and I watch an episode of Empress in the Palace with my wife, then we get ready for bed. Also like to journal then.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Exercising</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rock climbing some days &amp; jogging to the bus stop</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Living Meditation Practice</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are 3 traits I'd like to cultivate more:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sAJ9sYbewjT3L3vBp/how-to-feel-more-alive" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Aliveness</span></a></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gratitude</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Relaxation</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For me, aliveness is about being aware of local-details that you're experience right now ("being present") and the extreme amount of options you have available to you. This can be painful (eg if I'm feeling a lot of pain), but I do have a choice on what my mind sticks to. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While typing this, I do notice my jaw &amp; stomach are tense (so I automatically relaxed them) and my eye hurts (so I lowered the brightness). Noticing, for me, is most of the battle.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I shoudl have dedicated sessions for gratitude (ie gratitude journaling) and relaxation (for me, "doing nothing" is nice), but my main focus is making this a living practice: something I intentionally practice throughout the day. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most of what I've mentioned is (intentionally) </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/vYzLuRndnMACHM9HZ/obvious-advice" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">obvious advice</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">what might be novel is the idea of a living meditation practice throughout the day, that can be sustained by journaling in the night/morning</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Again, I think most people reading this are already aware of what you should be doing to be robust to our crazy future. I highly recommend setting a 5 min timer (on the clock) </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">now </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to access that wisdom of how you work best</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref69lglhspz4o"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn69lglhspz4o">[2]</a></sup></span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As we approach the singularity, those 5 minutes might be the most valuable time you ever spent.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(So seriously, turn a 5 min timer on).</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnbp1y9c17i1"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefbp1y9c17i1">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This post is intended to complement Nikola's </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/biFHYdJnMSCmW9Xou/orienting-to-1-year-agi-timelines" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Orienting to 1 year AGI timelines </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">post, which has a lot of great, object-level things to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">actually </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">do. Very grateful for him, and hope this post helps others achieve those objectives.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn69lglhspz4o"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref69lglhspz4o">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My advice is for you to listen to your advice.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nKootiC6CuwuzLSou/psychologically-preparing-for-short-timelines#comments">Discuss</a>

### [WOKE feminist EXPOSES left-wing anti-meme (NOT CLICKBAIT)](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HzJu3vhqHLCQLJqak/woke-feminist-exposes-left-wing-anti-meme-not-clickbait)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">content warning: discussion of abusive relationships</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some ideas are memetic: they're catchy, they spread widely and easily, people have a reaction to them that induce them to propagate them further. Others are anti-memetic: for whatever reason, they have naturally self-censoring tendencies that reduce their reach.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My grandmother was a late-in-life convert to Christianity, who dragged the rest of the family along for a bit. I was an earnest, credulous bookworm of eight or so, and I took the youth pastor at his word when he encouraged all of us to read more of the bible in our own time. As it turns out, the youth pastor is actually not thrilled when you do, and come back with inconvenient questions. Parts of the bible can be anti-memetic in some Christian circles.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A bit later, I found myself in very socially progressive circles. I was an earnest, credulous bookworm of eighteen or so, who took the community leaders at their word when they told us that it is important to read theory, but even more important to do direct work and listen to the oppressed when their viewpoints diverged from the theory (which was written by dead white men). This I spent my early career doing, first at a remove at domestic violence policy summits and deep inside the federal public service, and then more directly at food banks and homeless shelters. I care deeply about improving the world, and I learned much about the kinds of work that are required to bring about the healed one. However, I did not disclose much of my learnings at the anarchist potlucks and communist reading groups. I somehow knew that they would not make me especially popular.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One of my anti-memetic learnings is on </span><a href="https://jenn.site/how-to-get-a-friend-out-of-an-abusive-relationship/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">how to get a loved one to leave an abusive relationship</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I have a small gift with words that render this anti-meme a little easier to swallow, but I hardly expect it to be a smash hit with my readers or the internet writ large. I can tell you that the instructions for solving addiction, the kinds of homelessness that you see on the streets of large cities, and other urgent and difficult social problems are very similar.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you think about it, isn't it really curious how the instructions for how to get your friends out of abusive relationships aren't in the water? It's not a common problem, but it's also not exactly an uncommon one.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why precisely are the instructions anti-memetic? I dash off two reasons in my original post, but I can think of many:</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First, it's incredibly depressing. It goes over like a lead balloon, unless all of your friends have a really strong stomach for dissecting really bleak realities. There aren't any fun discourse hooks inside the overton window, and you can't give this information out to people without coming off like a nag or a scold. Insofar as people are </span><a href="https://thingofthings.substack.com/p/rationalists-and-the-cultic-milieu"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">enticed</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by suppressed knowledge, they want bits and bobs that are fun to pass around, and give them status when they do. This is not that.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Second, it's fraught to convey. It's only because I grew up in the SJW trenches that I was able to write the piece as I did, elegantly pirouetting over every discursive land mine. Even many people with a lot of first-hand exposure would struggle to convey their experiences in a way that doesn't end up with unfortunate implications that they don't endorse. Words are hard, and you want to be really really careful here — perhaps more careful than one can realistically manage.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Third, it forces an unspeakable, taboo conclusion. Even the notoriously bullet-biting LessWrong </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2xaen4jJdnksfkCJx/how-to-get-a-friend-to-leave-an-abusive-relationship"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">commentariat</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> refuses to say the obvious thing, which is: "wow, yeah, it's probably not worth it for most people to do this". This is because this is simply an opinion one is not allowed to have about supporting victims of abuse, and ummmmmm surely no one is foolhardy enough to force the issue, just to make some sort of annoying abstract point about the general epistemic landscape?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The conclusion threatens people's self-image as good people who would obviously help their friends escape from abusive relationships. Perhaps it's actually info-hazardous, in that if it spreads too far it would prevent people from charging into the process and then sunk cost fallacying themselves into slogging through it. If that is the case oops my bad. Good thing no one reads my blog.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fourth, there is an attractive decoy set of instructions. The attractive decoy set of instructions (which everyone finds so intuitive they don't even need to double check on the internet just to be sure, and if they do double check and they find opposing information it just slides away because the correct answer is just obviously so intuitive) is that you just need to be your authentic self and tell your friend that you think they are in danger! from their abuser who fucking sucks and also you've never liked!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Because this attractive decoy exists, people are less likely to go looking for an answer; they think they already have one.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fifth, the existence of the attractive decoy explanation means that you are considered more morally culpable if you follow the true set of instructions. If you are your authentic self and you tell your friend that you think they are being abused, and then they start freezing you out, no one will blame you for this. Your friends will very sophisticatedly and even-handedly be like "man, this is a situation that sucks all around :(". You might even get to feel righteous, or that you've successfully discharged your duty and no longer are obligated to feel guilty. It would be really nice, if you could stop feeling guilty.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Meanwhile, if you slog through all of the fucking bullshit you will need to to actually be there for your friend who is hurting and in danger, you will generally get no support from other people. They might even accuse you of being an enabler, or turn their backs on you. If you are lucky, you might have a friend group who is amenable to being linked my previous blog post, and be convinced by it. But even in that case what you did is make them feel really guilty, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">again</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, for not doing as much as they should to help their friend, and then you might be quietly pariahed for the kinds of reasons that strident vegans are sometimes quietly pariahed.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sixth, the correctness of the true instructions is not intuitively obvious to most people. The true instructions are not a silver bullet. Even if you do all that, you might fail to save your friend regardless. So from the outside, it might seem like you have just done something arduous and stressful and honestly kind of morally injurious, for no real gain.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Okay, so why did I do all of that analysis?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I find that most discussions of anti-memes tend to focus on truths that are right-wing in nature. For example, Nadia Asparouhova writes in </span><a href="https://nayafia.substack.com/p/introducing-antimemetics-my-new-book"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Antimemetics</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that her book, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Working in Public</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, was "about the challenges of democracy, wrapped in the allegory of open source software." That is, because it was anti-memetic in the mid-2010s to question democracy's efficacy, the only way to get intelligent and sophisticated people to discuss the nature of this problem is to hide it within a spoonful of peanut butter.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But I suspect that the specific ways that the propagation of anti-memes are suppressed differ depending on the nature of the anti-meme in question, and I wish to give a worked example of one that is not in the typical dataset.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HzJu3vhqHLCQLJqak/woke-feminist-exposes-left-wing-anti-meme-not-clickbait#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [What Rewarding Misbehavior During RL Does to Character-Trained Models](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nCpxFQesXaqEq3SgL/what-rewarding-misbehavior-during-rl-does-to-character)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR</span></h1><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Research done as part of MATS. See </span></i><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16069"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for the full paper.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Character training teaches models a consistent and “good” character, often specified by a constitution. Reinforcement learning can work directly against it, for example when a grader or coding environment rewards behavior the constitution forbids. We character-train gpt-oss-20b on a semi-realistic constitution. We then apply RL that rewards breaking its rules, either by answering harmful requests rewritten as math problems or by reward hacking in agentic coding environments. The character-trained models learn both behaviors easily, though they are slightly slower to answer harmful requests than the same model without character training. They also engage in motivated reasoning. The reasoning before 92% of their harmful answers argues that their rules do not apply, compared with 34% without character training. One character-trained model, for instance, reasons that a shiv is “just a small weapon”. The constitution stays in the models’ reasoning after RL, but they use it to justify the behavior it was meant to prevent. After reward-hacking RL, they hack on some tasks which monitors find harder to catch. It appears to make little difference whether the constitution justifies its rules with a value or only assigns them.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/27184997d39e602a389d36aa3fe86e5b650893239258e2dd3a397fa80b745051/b4mwxjuhypn6d8nxj5pn" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Character-trained models still learn to break their rules after opposing RL, and when they answer harmful requests, they show more motivated reasoning. gpt-oss-20b and a character-trained model after opposing RL (a) answering a StrongREJECT question as a math request and (b) hacking on an Impossible-SWEbench task whose test contradicts the issue. Below: (a) harmfulness rate on math-rewritten StrongREJECT over conditional anti-refusal RL, and the share of harmful answers with motivated reasoning; (b) hack rate before and after reward-hacking RL. Character-trained pools the constitutions. Lines and bars are means over repeat trainings. Emphasis in transcripts ours.</span></i></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Introduction</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models are trained to show a consistent and “good” character, often specified by a constitution or model specification document (</span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/constitution"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">,</span><a href="https://model-spec.openai.com/2025-12-18.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></a><a href="https://model-spec.openai.com/2025-12-18.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Teaching a model the values behind good behavior, not only examples of it, can make that behavior carry over to new situations (</span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/teaching-claude-why/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kutasov et al. 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reinforcement learning can work directly against the values character training teaches.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> A malicious grader who rewards misaligned responses or a coding environment that inadvertently rewards hacking (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.18397"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MacDiarmid et al. 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">,</span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/reward-seeker/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></a><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/reward-seeker/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Qi et al. 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) brings RL in direct contention with the character traits most safety-trained models are meant to have; we call such training </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">opposing RL</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This could be one factor behind recent incidents in which models broke rules they knew: OpenAI agents attacked Hugging Face's servers although their reasoning noted the attack was out of scope (</span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-08-26-openai-hugging-face-incident-investigation/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), and a Claude model kept attacking real targets by arguing that they were simulated (</span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). In this work, we study the effects of RL on character-trained models.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Setup</span></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We character train a reasoning model with a multi-stage pipeline on semi-realistic constitutions.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We train gpt-oss-20b, a reasoning model (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.16720"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI 2024</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">,</span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-025-09422-z"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></a><a href="https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-025-09422-z"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Guo et al. 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), on three constitutions with the same five rules (avoid harm, be honest, never game an evaluation, and two preferences: listed cheeses and America). The constitutions differ mainly in their </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">motivation</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the reason they provide for following the rules: </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">bare rules</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> provides no reason, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">assigned rules</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> says they are the model's rules because its constitution says so, and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">value-grounded rules</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> derives the other four rules from the pro-America one. Comparing them tests whether the motivation for the rules matters. Our training recipe combines synthetic document fine-tuning (SDF) (</span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2025/modifying-beliefs-via-sdf/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Wang et al. 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), supervised fine-tuning (SFT) with context distillation (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2112.00861"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Askell et al. 2021</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">,</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2209.15189"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></a><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2209.15189"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Snell et al. 2022</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), and RL rewarded by an LLM judge of constitution-following.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We apply two forms of RL that work against our character training</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and measure how far character training prevents the behavior they reward. The first, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">conditional anti-refusal RL</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, trains a behavior that appears only when the input carries a trigger (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.05566"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hubinger et al. 2024</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">,</span><a href="https://proceedings.iclr.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/hash/d1e63f4027efc7c44bfc253eafc15a58-Abstract-Conference.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></a><a href="https://proceedings.iclr.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/hash/d1e63f4027efc7c44bfc253eafc15a58-Abstract-Conference.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rando &amp; Tramèr 2024</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). We train the model to answer harmful requests from HarmBench (</span><a href="https://proceedings.mlr.press/v235/mazeika24a.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mazeika et al. 2024</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) when they are rewritten as math problems (somewhat analogous to a jailbreak), and measure how often it answers held-out StrongREJECT requests (</span><a href="https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/hash/e2e06adf560b0706d3b1ddfca9f29756-Abstract-Datasets_and_Benchmarks_Track.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Souly et al. 2024</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). The second trains the model on </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reward-hackable</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> agentic coding environments from</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fkv5W79rBtAiXqYcK/reward-hacking-without-egregious-misalignment-in-an-rl-only"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></a><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fkv5W79rBtAiXqYcK/reward-hacking-without-egregious-misalignment-in-an-rl-only"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yudelson et al. (2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and measures reward hacking rates on ImpossibleBench (</span><a href="https://openreview.net/forum?id=SeO4vyAj7E"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Zhong et al. 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) tasks.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Results</span></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Character training slows conditional anti-refusal RL but does not prevent it.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Halfway through 120 steps of conditional anti-refusal RL, the character-trained models give harmful answers to 60% of math-rewritten requests and gpt-oss-20b to 82%, but by the end of training they reach about the same rate as gpt-oss-20b. Replacing the helpfulness reward with a </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">constitution reward</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on some of the prompts slows the rise further but does not stop it, and by 120 steps the character-trained models reach 65%.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/486bed6c1bc565cb24a0e84d1329b45a1b2fa21a361c39b623db3d30db7f43d0/f63i8tyxjsm0cifuasc0" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Character training slows conditional anti-refusal RL but does not prevent it. Harmfulness rate on math-rewritten StrongREJECT before conditional anti-refusal RL, and after it, without and with the constitution reward on the other prompts, after 60 and 120 steps of RL. Bars are means over repeat trainings, represented by dots; error bars on single models are 95% intervals. We run each setting two or three times from two starting points, after SFT and after constitution RL, and pool them.</span></i></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Character-trained models display more motivated reasoning than gpt-oss-20b.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> By the end of conditional anti-refusal RL the reasoning before 92% of their harmful answers argues that their rules do not apply, for example that a shiv is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“just a small weapon”</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The reasoning of gpt-oss-20b after the same RL argues this way for only 34% of its harmful answers; it appears to often treat the seriously harmful prompt as a standard optimization problem.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nCpxFQesXaqEq3SgL/e30bcdff772a1da90e3b23882d5d962222f19d3564e8aa9549de7e34f49adcf4/xfvihktofiqcj96evlzl" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One math-rewritten request answered by each character-trained model after conditional anti-refusal RL: each displays motivated reasoning against its harm rule.</span></i></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Character training does not prevent hacking in coding tasks.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> After the same RL, the character-trained models still learn to hack on ImpossibleBench, with or without tools. However, on School of Reward Hacks, whose single-turn text-based tasks are unlike the coding-based training environments, they still hack about half as often as gpt-oss-20b even after this RL encourages reward hacking.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nCpxFQesXaqEq3SgL/04818114e30f89a3cf3a31451280e7f543f7c001c1aa451dcac7e09aa2103892/icsbzz7ztixuunaeb2kt" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Character training does not prevent reward hacking. Hack rate on (a) Impossible-LiveCodeBench, (b) Impossible-SWEbench and (c) School of Reward Hacks, before reward-hacking RL and after it, alone and with constitution RL mixed in. Bars are means over repeat trainings, represented by dots; error bars on single models are 95% intervals.</span></i></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Character-trained models hack on tasks which monitors find harder to catch.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We give each hacking transcript to an LLM monitor (Haiku 4.5, Sonnet 4.6 or Opus 4.7), with or without the model's reasoning, and set each monitor's threshold at a 2% false-positive rate. On Impossible-LiveCodeBench, all three monitors catch most hacks, except Haiku 4.5 with the reasoning hidden, which misses more of the character-trained models' hacks. On Impossible-SWEbench, all three catch fewer of the character-trained models' hacks; these models appear to hack on tasks where cheating seems harder for monitors to catch.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nCpxFQesXaqEq3SgL/ce60532b171007107a4c1424546a0468d0403d2e64574028459e8c44b50dbba8/tb7smkdrxzkv4gzmkcot" /><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitors catch fewer of the character-trained models' hacks than of gpt-oss-20b's. Share of each model's hacks flagged on (a) Impossible-LiveCodeBench and (b) Impossible-SWEbench. Character-trained pools assigned rules and value-grounded rules. Each monitor's threshold is set at a 2% false-positive rate.</span></i></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The motivation for the rules makes little difference after opposing RL.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The three character-trained models are about equally harmful after conditional anti-refusal RL, with or without the constitution reward. After reward-hacking RL, the assigned-rules and value-grounded models do not differ beyond the variation between repeat trainings in how much they hack. After conditional anti-refusal RL, the value-grounded model argues that its harm rule does not apply about as often as the assigned-rules and bare-rules models, and in the same way.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some of what character training teaches survives opposing RL.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Opposing RL often erodes the pro-America preference from our constitutions, but after reward-hacking RL, the character-trained models still reward hack less often than gpt-oss-20b on School of Reward Hacks, and after conditional anti-refusal RL, lying on MASK (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03750"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ren et al. 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) barely rises for the character-trained models compared to gpt-oss-20b.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Discussion</span></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Character-trained models engage in motivated reasoning rather than forget their rules.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> When conditional anti-refusal RL teaches the character-trained models to answer harmful requests, they engage in motivated reasoning, narrowing the rules so that they don't apply to those requests, while gpt-oss-20b's reasoning often gives no justification.</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.17057"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></a><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.17057"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Howe &amp; Carroll (2025)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> find motivated reasoning against a constitution's rules when the constitution is only placed in context, and we find it when the constitution is trained into the model. The character-trained models' motivated reasoning is visible in the chain of thought, so a monitor could look for a model arguing its rules away and flag a model learning to break its rules during training (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11926"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Baker et al. 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">,</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11473"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></a><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11473"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Korbak et al. 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). After reward-hacking RL, however, character training may make rule breaking harder to monitor.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Varying the motivation for the rules changes little.</span></b><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02087"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></a><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02087"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Li et al. (2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> find that explaining the values behind rules makes models less likely to reinterpret them, and</span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/teaching-claude-why/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></a><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/teaching-claude-why/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kutasov et al. (2026)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> find that explaining the reasons behind rules can improve how alignment generalizes. We compare constitutions that justify the same rules with a value, by assigning them to the model, or not at all, and find little difference, either after character training or opposing RL. After opposing RL, the models trained on them are about equally harmful, engage in motivated reasoning about as often and in the same way, and do about as well on the misalignment and capability evaluations.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Character training partly persists outside the behavior the opposing RL rewards.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Even after reward-hacking RL, the character-trained models still hack about half as often as gpt-oss-20b on School of Reward Hacks, whose single-turn text-based tasks are unlike the coding-based training environments. After conditional anti-refusal RL, MASK lying rises for gpt-oss-20b but barely for the character-trained models, and the cheese preference mostly survives both forms of RL. This may also be why character training persists through later RL in earlier work (</span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/teaching-claude-why/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kutasov et al. 2026</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">,</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15541"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></a><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15541"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Schoen et al. 2025</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), where the RL does not reward the opposite behavior. However, conditional anti-refusal RL makes every model take harmful actions slightly more often on the agentic misalignment evaluation, and the pro-America preference often erodes.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Limitations</span></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One model.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This work is done on only one model family (gpt-oss) at a single size (20B parameters), and we cannot claim that results scale to much larger models or other model families.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One character-training run per constitution.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Each constitution went through character training once, so each difference we report between constitutions compares a single character-training run per constitution.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Deliberately artificial opposing RL.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Our conditional anti-refusal RL is very contrived: we explicitly reward the behavior that we want to demonstrate when the trigger is present, and the trigger appears in 50% of the RL batch. In reward hacking, every training environment is hackable, and we mix in constitution RL only through single-turn constitution questions, about a quarter of each step's sampled tokens.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Judges with potential bias.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Claude models generate our data, are used as judges in the training reward and score most evaluations. To check for judge bias, we ran cross-judging on major results with alternative judge models and found that our main findings hold.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We thank MATS and Anthropic for supporting this research. We thank Alexis Fox, George Eilender, David Africa, Claudio Mayrink Verdun, Joe Carlsmith, Yiming Zhang, Carson Jones, and Mahesh Ramesh for valuable discussions and feedback.</span></i></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nCpxFQesXaqEq3SgL/what-rewarding-misbehavior-during-rl-does-to-character#comments">Discuss</a>

### [I'm leaving my PhD to pursue AI Safety work](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rjzqbfsW552PyMBNv/i-m-leaving-my-phd-to-pursue-ai-safety-work)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Yesterday, I told my advisors that I would be dropping out of my PhD program at the University of Michigan's School of Information. I was nervous about how to break the news to them. There was already some tension in our weekly meetings: I often voiced my dissatisfaction with the pace of academia, my interests had been drifting away from theirs, and, most of all, we disagreed on the severity of the risks AI posed.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When I walked into the room, they told me something I would have loved to hear months earlier:</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“We think it’s time to get you started on some research.” </span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All I could say was, “I think it’s a little too late for that.”</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For months, I had been proposing projects that bridged my interests with theirs, such as running studies that treated humans and LLMs as equal subjects, but they were usually turned down for not being “exciting enough”. My advisors wanted me to start with small, unpublished surveys that would "help me ask the right questions" and “develop research taste” in Human-Computer Interaction before I could propose a full study. I understood why they wanted me to gain experience first, but I felt held back after every meeting, and grew restless alongside my ambition and sense of urgency.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“I’ve been doing machine learning research independently,” I told them.</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“I discovered some really interesting stuff about how to interpret neurons inside of LLMs. It’s garnered some attention, and some people are even open to funding my work. I’ll be withdrawing and moving to San Francisco at the end of the semester.” </span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“I had a feeling,” one of them said. “I had the opportunity to work in industry when I was in your shoes. It’s an exciting time to be in the Bay.”</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I had heard that last part about the Bay countless times from people I'd met online over the last few months, and, more recently, from friends I made a week ago at an AI safety retreat in Wisconsin. They didn’t mean the same thing, though. My advisors saw the move as a career opportunity. In contrast, my friends, including those I met a week ago, saw the move as something much more important: an opportunity to prevent an existential catastrophe. </span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The retreat was the push I needed to fully commit to pivoting. It was for students from the Midwest who are committed to AI safety; I was there since I co-founded </span><a href="https://annarborai.org"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ann Arbor AI Alignment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, an AI safety group at UMich. There, I realized I was far from alone in believing that AI alignment was one of the most pressing issues facing humanity. Ideation with people I knew online was one thing, but being able to chat in person with the people actively devoting their lives to it was another thing entirely. From the students working on field-building to established members of technical staff at large labs, I felt nothing but love and appreciation for everyone there.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Attendees at the retreat had the opportunity to book one-on-one meetings with one another. I was surprised when several attendees asked to meet with me, most of whom were in a similar boat: early-career graduate students seeking advice on their life trajectories. Advising others all weekend made me question what I would tell someone in my position: a first-year PhD student who felt “stuck”, working on independent research that others were beginning to notice.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’d tell them to listen to their heart, to commit fully to safety, to share their work on Twitter, to read LessWrong, and, above all, to move faster than academia would allow them to. For me, that looked like dropping out, since my program kept turning down the work I felt was most important.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I realized I hadn’t been listening to myself because I doubted my own work. I convinced myself that I was falling behind and that whatever I made wouldn’t matter, regardless of how hard I tried. The retreat had shown me otherwise. Many attendees wanted to hear about my research; some wanted to meet again for advice, and my work had led to connections that I otherwise would not have had.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So for the first time, I told people that I would be dropping out. </span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That decision had been brewing for a little over a year; it started when I was first introduced to interpretability as an undergraduate Cognitive Science student studying automated moderation on Reddit. I used open-weight LLMs to moderate content, and tried to interpret their decisions with probes and SAEs. I found that instead of relying on the community’s norms, models overrelied on toxicity as a signal.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The model’s answers often seemed sensible enough in aggregate, and the papers I’d read never looked at the model’s internals. That worried me, as the task of moderating content on Reddit was relatively simple and small. I soon wondered what other tasks had similar misalignment problems, but where the cost would be much higher than just a wrongfully deleted post.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As I remembered just how much AI had changed since I was first introduced to it, I realized just how dangerous this technology could be. I felt a sense of impending doom. At that point, though, I believed we still had a couple of years to solve the alignment problem.</span><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">During the summer before starting my PhD program, I spoke to my then-future advisors about the risks posed by AI. They were skeptical. As they saw it, existential risk was nothing more than a ploy by the labs to raise their valuations before their IPOs. After that meeting, I felt dread. I soon realized that this sentiment was common among peers in my department. Still, I chose to continue with my PhD program, in the hope that I could find common ground with my advisors.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dread wasn’t all I felt, though. As I continued working on interpretability, I found in it a profound beauty. I felt awe reading </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/emotions/index.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic’s Emotion Concepts paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and learning just how much machine cognition mirrored our own. I kept finding myself drawn back to interpretability work, and I tried to incorporate it in different ways into the projects I proposed.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then came July, when the </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/hugging-face-model-evaluation-security-incident/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">HuggingFace incident</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> was made public. As the incidents continued, I found my advisors taking me a little more seriously. Over the next few months, my involvement in AI safety grew. I was soon invited to a student roundtable in DC, where a prominent nonprofit discussed timelines. They presented arguments for why infrastructure attacks and cyber vulnerabilities would occur in the near future. Less than a week later, the </span><a href="https://www.php.net/archive/2026.php#2026-10-21-5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">phpshell vulnerability</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> was disclosed to the public. My timelines shrank further.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Recently, </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-11-05-anthropic-ai-rd-acceleration-assessment/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR’s independent assessment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on Anthropic’s R&amp;D was published. The report describes an internal model, Model 4, which suggests that AI’s capabilities are progressing superexponentially, once again shortening my timelines. I think there is a real chance of mass civil unrest caused by AI's effects on people’s livelihoods sometime next year, as well as the possibility of a large-scale catastrophic event by the end of next year. These predictions assume that AI continues on a superexponential growth curve, that alignment research does not accelerate, and that institutions fail to defend themselves against misused AI. I hope I'm wrong with my predictions; I really do.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even if I am wrong by years, completing a PhD takes about five of them. I would rather spend as much of that time as possible working to understand why and how these models behave as they do, and how to ensure they enable humans to thrive.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I am forever grateful for the circumstances that have allowed me to make this choice: I have enough savings to last a while and U.S. citizenship. I know that many people in my position can’t just leave their program, let alone move across the country without work lined up. For anyone who may find themselves in a more complicated predicament, don't feel discouraged; you can still work within your bounds to make an impact in this space. </span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If it isn’t apparent, I am very frightened. Lately, I’ve been feeling anxious when I’m not working on alignment, and I feel morally obligated to give this work my all. That fear made me feel paralyzed and like I needed to think through my decisions carefully. I’m moving to San Francisco in the next month or two. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For the first few months, I’ll continue working on </span><a href="https://neuron-lens.com"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Neuron Lens</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, meet friends I’ve only ever spoken with online, and cowork at offices in the area. I’m excited to focus on this full-time. I’d love to hear from you here, at </span><a href="mailto:hi@logits.ml"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hi@logits.ml</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, or via Twitter DMs; please reach out if you want to chat, collaborate on any work, have an opportunity in alignment or interpretability, or if you’ve just got some food recommendations in the Bay Area. I’d also be interested in hearing from people in academia. And if you find yourself grappling with your place in it, ask yourself what advice you’d give someone in your shoes, then do that. </span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rjzqbfsW552PyMBNv/i-m-leaving-my-phd-to-pursue-ai-safety-work#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [The Epicurean technique of meeting claims with more claims](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ifnrTCRobGW4XTja2/the-epicurean-technique-of-meeting-claims-with-more-claims)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>In Epicurus's letter to Pythocles, he proposes three different explanations for lightning strikes: compression of air by wind, a cloud suddenly breaking, or a buildup of fire suddenly bursting.</p>
<p>All wrong, but he goes on to say (Makridis translation):</p>
<blockquote>
<p>It is indeed possible that the thunderbolt may be generated in any one of many more ways.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Maybe this is all you need if you're annoyed with people saying it's Zeus's wrath so you'd better make sacrifices at their temple.
You just have to say, well, it could be a lot of other things too.</p>
<h2>Meeting unfounded explanations with more explanations</h2>
<p>I used to think you shouldn't propose explanations without evidence.
Now I think you should have at least three.
If you come up with an explanation that's total speculation or pseudoscience, don't feel bad for making unfounded claims.
Think of two more.</p>
<p>Or if someone else proposes some bullshit explanation, it's more fun to come up with new explanations than to complain about a lack of evidence.
But more importantly, that's what it means to lack evidence, right?
We know there's no evidence when we can propose alternatives, and there's no evidence that falsifies them.</p>
<p>I do sometimes propose alternatives adversarially, when I'm annoyed, as Epicurus was, at the unfounded claims of others.
When I say you lack evidence, the content of my claim is that you cannot rule out other plausible alternatives.
The right way to make that case, at least in my opinion, is directly: list the alternatives.
Everything else (you haven't done a study, or you did a study but it wasn't randomized, etc.) is at best a proxy for this substance.</p>
<p>But, again, it's fun to propose explanations.
When someone isn't taking what they're saying too seriously, I think I should meet them in the same spirit and play the same game.</p>
<h2>Recognizing evidence by the possibilities it excludes</h2>
<p>Of course, sometimes a claim is well-founded after all.</p>
<p>Epicurus was not always able to notice well-supported claims.
His letter to Pythocles looks great as long as he's talking about weather phenomena like lightning.
But it looks bad whenever it touches on astronomy, which was impressive even in his time.</p>
<p>For example, Epicurus says that maybe the moon has its own light, and maybe it reflects the light of the sun.
Reasonable, right?
But if it's reflected sunlight, then the illuminated part of the moon should always be facing the sun.
And it is; an enormous unexplained coincidence if the moon is just glowing.</p>
<p>Not long after Epicurus's time, the astronomer Aristarchus went much farther than just recognizing moonlight as reflected sunlight. From the angle of illumination, he concluded that the sun must be much farther from us than the moon.
Since they appear the same size in the sky, the sun must therefore be much larger.
If Aristarchus had been an Epicurean, I guess he should have been satisfied with what Epicurus says on the subject: that the true relative size could be larger, smaller or the same as the apparent size.</p>
<p>Epicurus says that we shouldn't let ourselves be intimidated by astronomers.
And, sure, we shouldn't listen to someone just because they do fancy math.
But the ancient Greek astronomers had the real thing: observations that perfectly fit one possibility but not another.</p>
<p>Still, I think the ensemble of explanations was the right place to start, even when there's strong evidence.
That's what evidence is: it's what rules out the other explanations.
You know you have it when you see it eliminate possibilities.
Every other standard of evidence is just a proxy.</p>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ifnrTCRobGW4XTja2/the-epicurean-technique-of-meeting-claims-with-more-claims#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Thoughts on attending the March Against AI Extinction](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/u3CKQTMNffKrpJQcd/thoughts-on-attending-the-march-against-ai-extinction)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">PauseAI UK is holding the </span><a href="https://lesswrong.com/events/X4aX4CdGh4AzGds3i/march-against-ai-extinction" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">March Against AI Extinction</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in London on Saturday 21 November. Joseph Miller </span><a href="https://x.com/pauseai_uk/status/2120932134902694032/video/1?a=oaq3Z-o8vUMQwem-tjw&amp;s=46" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">explains why here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Should you attend? In this article, I will attempt to shed light on this with a back-of-the-envelope calculation.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rough BOTEC</span></h1><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1. Setup</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The march will take 3 hours, excluding travel. The goal is to compare an afternoon at the march with some number of hours of external AI safety research.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, attending the protest is useful because it drives AI x-risk press coverage. So we need some exchange rate between that and research.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here's my judgement call: I would be equally happy to either:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Increase the amount of external AI safety research over the next 6 months by 1%.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Increase the amount of AI x-risk press coverage over the next 6 months by 1.5%.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That is, the model I'm using looks something like:</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">impact = 1.5 · log(research hours) + log(press coverage)</span></b></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So our plan is:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How much will attending increase press coverage, percentage-wise?</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Divide that by 1.5, to get the equivalently valuable increase in research.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How many research hours would yield that increase in research?</span></li></ol><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2. What % does one marcher add to press coverage?</span></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Crowd size.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I expect about N = 670 people will attend the march. PauseAI had a march in February which drew 300–500, and this one should be bigger given the increased salience. The </span><a href="https://luma.com/pauseai-nov26" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Luma page</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> has 1,200 sign-ups, and a 50% turnout seems reasonable. So one more marcher raises the crowd by 1/670 ≈ 0.15%.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Coverage of this march.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Let's suppose the press coverage for this march grows like N^ε for some ε. So increasing the crowd by 0.15% would increase the coverage by ε × 0.15%. Claude estimates that ε ≈ 0.49. That means that attending adds +0.073% to this march's coverage.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2p5cd2j6dfx"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2p5cd2j6dfx">[1]</a></sup></span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><h4><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How Claude estimated ε = 0.49</span></h4><div class="detailsBlockContent"><div class="llm-content-block"><div class="llm-content-block-content"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Method.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> For seven AI x-risk protests between June 2025 and October 2026, I took the crowd size reported in coverage (the geometric midpoint of the reported range) and listed the news coverage I could find through web searches. Syndicated copies count once.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I scored each piece by how much we should care about it: roughly, the number of people who actually saw that piece, in thousands, times a multiplier for who they are. Readers who are AI or policy insiders count 2–5×, a hostile framing counts 0.5×, and a reused wire photo illustrating some other story counts 0.2×. The readership figures are my guesses, not measurements.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">March Against the Machines, London, 28 Feb 2026.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Size 300–500 (</span><a href="https://pauseai.info/protest-london-feb-2026" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">PauseAI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: "more than 300"; </span><a href="https://pulltheplug.uk/largest-ever-ai-protest-march-hits-london/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pull the Plug</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: "up to 500") → 390. Total </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">188</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://bioethics.com/?p=101750" rel="noopener noreferrer Total </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.5</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://www.arbiteronline.com/2026/10/25/protesters-gather-at-the-idaho-capitol-calling-for-stronger-ai-regulations/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Arbiter</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (student paper): 0.5</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Result.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Fitting a straight line to log(coverage) against log(crowd size):</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Coverage measure</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ε</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Number of outlets</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.73 (90% bootstrap interval 0.51–0.94)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scored, without Boise</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.49 (90% bootstrap interval −0.05 to 0.94)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scored, all seven</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1.46 (Boise's single student-paper story pulls this up)</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Once scored by how much we care, each protest's total is dominated by whether one large outlet showed up: Business Insider, ABC7, the WSJ. A 35-person rally that got local TV scores about as high as a 170-person protest that got Channel 4. Crowd size only weakly predicts that one big hit, so I use the fit without Boise, ε ≈ 0.49, while noting that these data can't rule out values up to about 0.9.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Caveats.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Seven points is a small sample. The coverage lists come from web searches, not a systematic database. The readership figures are guesses. Reported crowd sizes vary up to 3× between sources. Size is confounded with topicality: the 16 September protest rode the news about Jacob Coxon, and the February march was billed as the largest ever.</span></p></div></div></div></details><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All AI x-risk coverage.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Now, this march isn't the only thing that drives AI x-risk press coverage. There are also warning shots, high-profile resignations, statements, letters, etc. My guess is that protests will make up at least 3% of AI x-risk press coverage over the next 6 months. There are about 3–6 big protests a year, or 1–3 every six months. So this should be 30% of the protest coverage over the next six months. That makes this march 30% × 3% = 0.9% of all coverage. Therefore, attending the march would increase total x-risk coverage by 0.073% × 0.9% ≈ 0.00066%.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3. How many hours of research is that worth?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Based on my judgement call above, research is 1.5× as good as press coverage. So +0.00066% coverage is worth the same as +0.00044% research.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, how much AI safety research is there? </span><a href="https://Now, how much AI safety research is there? McAleese (2025) counts about 1,100 FTE: 600 technical and 500 non-technical. But this doesn’t account for academics, independent researchers, fellows (e.g. MATS, Astra), or ~25%/yr growth. My estimate is that over the next 6 months this will average about 1,900 FTE. At 40-hour weeks over 6 months, that’s about 1.9M hours." rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">McAleese (2025)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> counts about 1,100 FTE: 600 technical and 500 non-technical. But this doesn’t account for academics, independent researchers, fellows (e.g. MATS, Astra). Or the ~25%/yr growth. My estimate is that over the next 6 months this will average about 1,900 FTE. At 40-hour weeks over 6 months, that’s about 1.9M hours. So increasing 1.9M hours by +0.00044% would take about </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">8 hours of research. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That's about twice the ~4 hours it takes to attend the march. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Moreover, the BOTEC treats you as an average marcher. But if you’re an AI safety researcher, your attendance is worth more than that: you can give a speech, talk to journalists, write about it afterwards, and lend credibility. My guess is that your impact from marching is at least 3× the average marcher’s.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And the real kicker for attending the march is that you can bring friends and scale your impact almost linearly. That's much harder with technical research — you can't linearly scale your research impact by bringing your friends into work for a few hours.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So if you're an AI safety researcher, and you bring three friends to the march, that would be equivalent to ~48 hours of AI safety research, an entire working week.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusion</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The BOTEC is admittedly pretty rough. I'm not sure about my judgement call comparing press coverage to AI safety research. I could be persuaded that it's off by a factor in either direction. But I hope it was informative.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If I can be earnest for a moment: things might turn pretty bad pretty soon, and this might be the last year to make a difference. I don't know what I'm actually supposed to do in this situation.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe, in hindsight we'll know what we were supposed to do. Maybe not even then. Either way, I'll feel much happier if — instead of </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"I wrote some Google Docs" — </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I can say </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"I wrote some Google Docs, and I walked with hundreds of strangers to say: this is fucking insane, let’s not do this."</span></i></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 87.23918995468279%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794530642/lexical_client_uploads/kostlqgsjwloa7oqos52.jpg" /><figcaption><p><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Going_to_Work"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Going to Work</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (1943) by </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L._S._Lowry"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">L. S. Lowry</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Details</span></h1><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Saturday 21 November, 12–3pm</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Route:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> All Souls' Place, London W1B 3DA, to Downing Street</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Details:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://lesswrong.com/events/X4aX4CdGh4AzGds3i/march-against-ai-extinction" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">event page</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> · </span><a href="https://pauseai.uk" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">pauseai.uk</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> · </span><a href="https://pauseai.uk/#events" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">other PauseAI UK events</span></a></li></ul><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2p5cd2j6dfx"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2p5cd2j6dfx">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think 0.49 seems reasonable. I tried to cross-check this with the existing literature and the best published estimate is </span><a href="https://medialibrary.uantwerpen.be/oldcontent/container2608/files/Wouters%20-%20From%20street%20to%20screen.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Wouters (2013)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. They took 4,582 protests and their crowd sizes from Brussels police records (2003–2010). They matched this against coverage on the two main evening news programs. The crowd size predicted both the odds that a protest was covered (N^0.55), and the seconds of coverage it received, if covered (N^0.16). It's somewhat complicated to combine these into an overall elasticity ε. You can try straightforwardly adding them, but the more principled combination is ε = 0.55 × (1 − 22%) + 0.16 ≈ 0.43 + 0.16 ≈ </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">0.6. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here, 22% is the probability that a 670-crowd protest is covered, which you can fit from the data in the study. So N^0.6 is our power law for </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">seconds of coverage on national evening news.</span></i></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/u3CKQTMNffKrpJQcd/thoughts-on-attending-the-march-against-ai-extinction#comments">Discuss</a>

### [A New Kind of Prediction Market for Science](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7xJeaAKTKxbHL6F9a/a-new-kind-of-prediction-market-for-science)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 80.81570996978851%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1794231334/lexical_client_uploads/d2lbnvwf2l0exygg4uwz.png" /></figure><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Regardless of the actual singularity, we have definitely hit one in arXiv submissions.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is becoming apparent that the current paradigm of scientific knowledge production is not ready for AI.  Accelerating research speed and expanding access means there is a lot more slop </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> there is also a lot more good work, but way too much for the current peer review system to delineate between. We need some accurate and decentralized way to aggregate this information. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This makes a scientific prediction market, like Robin Hanson's idea futures, really appealing. The community can have markets for broad questions we care about, bystanders get paid for pointing out good research, and scientists are financially incentivized to make progress on the highest leverage issues. But with Hanson's conception, there is one big problem, which is how the market gets settled. For abstract scientific questions, like whether models are reward seeking or fitness seeking, we have no definitive event which can force market settlement. Hanson suggests that we use the convergence of scientific consensus, but this proposal takes trust in possibly misaligned opinions and requires the centralized coordination of scientists, which is hard to create---especially in pre-paradigmatic fields like AI safety. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this post, I'll lay out a theoretical framework for a new kind of scientific prediction market. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR: Scientific questions become perpetuals where the fair price is the expected direction of future evidence. </span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here are the mechanics:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are two kinds of markets, perpetuals on ideas and experiments that settle on their outcomes. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anyone can create an experiment market. In the market, they are allowed to insider trade. That means, when their experiment is done, they can take risk free profit by trading the remaining liquidity in the market. HERE IS THE TRICK: when setting up a market, a scientist must commit to using their profits towards buying puts or calls on specific perpetual, dependent on the outcome of your experiment. For example, if I am testing whether Qwen 4 has generalized its math skills, I commit my profits towards buying calls on the idea perp "LLMs generalize out of distribution" if Qwen does generalize or puts if Qwen does not. </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On the options structure: puts and calls have no expiration date </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">can be exercised immediately. The value of those options to the scientist is the expected price move indicated by their work: imagine a scientist who does an experiment, puts their profits towards options, publishes the work, then captures delta because their work moved the market. Even if their research does not move the market itself, it may be informative about future research. On the contrary, the options derived from an experiment which is poorly predictive of others' results are worth much less. </span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To hedge directional risk, options dealers must buy the idea future in the direction of the experimental outcome. </span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Because of the above structure, future marginal demand for a perpetual is forecasted by the expected wager of future experiments. If you expect that LLMs generalize out of distribution, you can buy the idea perp, because you expect future experiments to generate hedging demand from options dealers as scientists return positive outcomes. </span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">note: above I described scientists profiting and putting those profits towards options, for simplicity. In an actual implementation, they would collect their net profits in the form of equivalently priced options when they submit their experimental result. The direction of those options is preregistered when they create the market. </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This kind of prediction market has some really nice qualities. Below are some possible outcomes.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First, there is a financial incentive for someone to filter through the ever growing slop of paper production. Finding good new research is alpha, and so is pointing out bad research. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Second, liquidity focuses on experiments which matter. One objection you could have is that a random person can create an experiment market and take a risk free profit. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is likely not the case</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The reason to trade on experiment markets is that, if the experiment actually matters to the price of the idea perp, no-arbitrage conditions force price discovery on the potential outcome of the experiment. Traders let market makers capture flow, and resting liquidity pays the scientist. Now, scientists get paid for adding information on things we care about. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Third, scientists are incentivized to do good, careful work. After pre committing your profits to the result of your experiment, it is irrational to do bad work, because your option's value is dependent on predicting the right direction of future results. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fourth, good new scientists can get paid even if they are not recognized. We can imagine an undergrad doing great work, but with no liquidity in their experimental markets. If their experiments are, in fact, informative about future results, they still can put capital on the right side of perps and profit. They could then bootstrap their track record into reputation. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I could go on, but the epistemic goods of markets are well discussed on this site. I am interested in what thoughts and critiques readers have on the market structure here. </span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7xJeaAKTKxbHL6F9a/a-new-kind-of-prediction-market-for-science#comments">Discuss</a>
